ParlaMint-DK_20160517131130	Does anyone wish to speak? Mrs. Mette Reissmann, the Social Democratic Party, go ahead.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517131146	On 7 November 2014, we witnessed a very good example of why there is every reason to vote against L 146. On 7 November 2014, the Danish company OW Bunker applied for bankruptcy. The bankruptcy petition came as a shock at the time. A few months before, OW Bunker had gone to the Stock Exchange with a terrific debut, the investors were queued up, the business was running as smeared. The truth, however, was not quite so rosy; on the contrary, a few months after the stock exchange listing, it turned out that the work was so creative with the bookkeeping that the balloon jumped with a bang. OW Bunker went bankrupt on 7 November 2014 and took many with him. It spreads large rings in the water when a company that had 2,900 customers in 134 countries during periods and which translated hundreds of millions of kroner a day goes bankrupt. The more than 600 employees employed in OW Bunker were fired, and nearly 20,000 individuals and companies who had invested in the company in the belief that they could rely on the accounting that had taken place now felt the consequences of the accounting in the hard way. Among the investors were several pension funds that invested ordinary, hardworking Danes' savings in a company that they rightly thought could be trusted. Then why is there any reason to tell the story of OW Bunker again? I assume that everyone who is here is familiar with it. That is because, as you know, the proposal enjoys the support of the majority in the blue bloc. So what's the point? The point is simple: OW Bunker's bankruptcy should be the expensive tuition that makes us here in the Folketing together ensure the necessary revision of the companies that are of great importance to society. OW Bunker went bankrupt because the accounts were messed up. The ultimate responsibility is not yet placed, but there is no doubt that the bankruptcy was triggered by creative accounting. We must avoid such situations for all things in the world. It hits Danish jobs, and it hits Danish growth. It therefore requires that we also have an audit system that we can trust. Unfortunately, we have to note that the legislative proposal under consideration now will have exactly the opposite effect. The government's light version of EU rules weakens the role and independence of auditors and, at worst, invites the OW Bunker-size scandals again. With the minimum implementation requested by the government, there are virtually no companies in Jutland that will be subject to the new rules. In particular, there are two parts of the draft law that will have harmful consequences. First, the government chooses to define public-interest companies, the PIE companies, as narrowly as possible. This also means that only listed companies, credit institutions and insurance companies are defined as PIE companies, which place stricter requirements on the audit. This is quite wrong, however, because in Denmark we do have a large number of companies that translate billions of kroner and hundreds of employees who are not affected by that exact definition. The definition includes, for example: not large fund-owned and unlisted public limited companies, family-owned companies or municipal and state companies. This applies, for example, to companies such as Velux, Grundfos, LEGO, ECCO and JYSK. They are of great importance in terms of jobs and local tax revenue. We have KMD, Nets and DONG, which have all or part of US fund owners. They fall outside, too. We have DSB, SEAS-NVE, HOFOR and Falck, which are important utilities. They're falling outside. Then we also have the stray money: the large concentration of capital in funds such as TrygFonden and Realdania. They're falling outside. I think it is obvious to everyone that it will have major social consequences if only one of the companies mentioned went bankrupt because of the roots in the accounts. Secondly, the draft law opens up a huge barn door for conflicts of interest in the context of the review. This also means that a company has to audit a customer's accounts for up to 70%. of the total fee paid by the company to the customer. This means that the other advisory services must amount to as much as 41 per cent. of the total fee, which is far too high. There is a conflict of interest built into the bill, and it is completely unnecessary to allow for that. That is why we also need to safeguard the independence of auditors. As a society, we cannot live with the fact that there can be doubts about the group that acts as representatives of the confidence of society vis-à-vis companies, and the draft law raises doubts about this confidence. At the same time, a number of key companies in our society are exempted. The government's proposal is supported by the Danish People's Party, the Liberal Alliance and the Conservatives. I do not want to interfere in how the four parties reach their policy, but I am nevertheless frankly surprised that the memory is so short that nothing has been learned from the OW Bunker Congress, from the Stein Bagger case, from the financial crisis that hurt Denmark so badly. On the political side, we just have to do everything we can to ensure the necessary and independent review of our large companies. Unfortunately, the proposal weakens confidence in the auditors and the audit of Danish key companies. I would therefore like to make a strong appeal to the four parties in the blue bloc to vote in favour of the amendments tabled by the Socialists together with the Radical Left. It's not too late to change your position. I would urge the government not to expose Danish industry to the great uncertainty of introducing vital business regulation with the narrowest possible majority. As far as the position of the Socialists is concerned, I would just like to say that we do not support a proposal that will ultimately play a gambling role with the crucial review of our businesses. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517132208	The debate is open. It's first the Minister of Business and Growth, here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517132240	The motion for a resolution called B 141 obliges the government to provide for a derogation which means that Danish mortgage credit institutions will not be covered by the future Basel IV capital requirement. The purpose of the resolution is, according to the proposers, to ensure that the expected future capital requirements for credit institutions do not lead to an increase in the contribution rates for borrowers in mortgage credit institutions. The future capital requirements of the Basel Committee are a matter which the government takes very seriously. That is why I was also recently at a meeting in Stockholm, where I met with the Chairman of the Basel Committee to present the Danish views, and the President showed understanding for the Danish points, which I among other things. at the meeting. I have also discussed the matter in depth with Commissioner Hill. I have done so both in the autumn and as recently as this spring. This was also a constructive and positive dialogue. In practice, however, the Basel Committee's standards for the regulation of credit institutions are only indicative and therefore there are no directly applicable requirements or rules from which credit institutions can be exempted. New capital requirements for the Basel Committee must be introduced into EU legislation before they become binding on EU credit institutions and therefore also on Danish mortgage institutions. However, it is not appropriate to exclude mortgage credit institutions in general from EU rules, since there are also advantages of being subject to a set of rules. Among these benefits is a more favourable status for the issue of mortgage bonds, which is a substantial part of the explanation for the low interest rate paid by borrowers. Therefore, the Government considers that neither institutions nor borrowers will benefit from the exclusion of mortgage credit institutions from EU rules. The government works instead of taking into account special business models such as Danish mortgage when implementing Basel standards in EU legislation through an early recognition of interests. This is happening, among other things. through ongoing contacts with other EU countries, the European Commission, the ECB and others. The government will continue to fight both in the European Union and internationally, to ensure that the Danish mortgage is taken into account, and in view of this and for the other reasons I have now mentioned, the government cannot support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517132526	In the comments to B 141 the proposers write that the Danish mortgage model is unique. I very much agree with the authors of the motion, and the Socialists fully agree that we in the Danish Parliament must work together to ensure our mortgage system. At the same time, however, I would like to make it clear that we need to be very precise in relation to the steps we are taking if we are really to secure our mortgage system. With this in mind, I can say at the outset that the Socialists do not support the Enhedslisten's motion for a resolution. However, before I elaborate on the background to this, I would like to stress that this does not mean that we are not very concerned about the widest possible cooperation on this issue. As I said, we need to take the right steps together if we are to secure the mortgage system. And in relation to the specific content of the proposal, the Socialists find the proposal problematic, because it will not be the solution to the challenges of our very system, just as it will only make it much more expensive to borrow money in the mortgage system. First of all, we must remember that the Basel Committee is not a legislative power from which Denmark can simply resign, because we are not a member. But even more importantly, it is the EU system that transforms the Basel Committee's standards in the financial area exactly into real legislation. And Denmark's relationship with the European Union obviously means that there is no possibility, so to speak, of ignoring legislation. In this way, it is hypothetical that, in order to follow the intention of the motion for a resolution, we should be outside the scope of EU regulation, and that would have very unfortunate consequences. If Denmark finds itself outside the EU regulation in this area, it will most likely mean a more difficult and not least more expensive access to finance for Danish home owners and companies. This is simply due to the fact that the low Danish mortgage prices are based on a very high degree of investor confidence in the Danish mortgage market. If Denmark did not, hypothetically, follow EU directives and regulations, international investors would become very skeptical of, among others. Danish mortgage bonds and translate it into that we have something to hide. That would be seen as clearly negative. Interest from foreign investors in investing in Danish mortgage bonds would dive significantly. This would increase the costs for homeowners and businesses because they would find it more difficult to finance their homes and businesses. So, as I mentioned at the outset, it is absolutely crucial that we in the Danish Parliament stand together when it comes to defending our unique and very well-functioning mortgage system. This is not the first time that it is under pressure that it is not the first time that it has been debated outside Denmark's borders. At that time, the solution was the same as it should be today. It is therefore a completely unanimous Parliament that must stand shoulder to shoulder in order simply to have a unified call from the Danish side for the mortgage system to maintain its unique structure. It makes an impression when a united Parliament makes such statements. That is why I think it is unfortunate that in this way there is a solo race on the part of the Enhedslisten with a resolution of this nature. It simply sends the wrong signals to the outside world. I have great respect for the intention behind the proposal, which I think most people in this Chamber also share, and for the same reason we should also stand behind the government when it tells the good story of the strong Danish mortgage system based on facts such as the historically low loss history of mortgage loans, as there are, and insist that that is what is being preserved. That is precisely what makes the crucial difference. I hope that Enhedslisten can also see it from that side, so that we can jointly secure the Danish mortgage system.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517133339	This motion for a resolution, tabled by Enhedslisten, follows on from the debate we had here in the Danish Parliament last week, on Friday, when we dealt with B 99, and also on the developments that have taken place in relation to the very much increasing rates of contributions and price cuts. It is, of course, the opposite that we would like to see, namely that the costs are as small as possible. In here we talk a lot about, for example. tax, and if there is a small tax increase, it is really something that can get many out of the chairs, and if you get a tax relief of DKK 100. a month, it is something that every government celebrates with pomp and splendor. However, many people who have received such a letter from a mortgage institution have lost hundreds of kroner a month in contributions rates. So this is a matter that we need to take seriously. That's one side of it. The other side of this is something we are really sorry about in Enhedslisten, and that is that these demands are of course instrumental in pushing forward a development where mortgage banks stop being what they have been in Danish history, namely customer-owned and customer-controlled mortgage institutions. It is something very unique about the Danish mortgage institutions, what many have talked about. They are actually owned by customers or owned by customers. Unfortunately, there is only one of them left; the rest has been swallowed up by large financial supermarkets, such as the United States. Danske Bank. Nykredit is still owned by Nykredit's customers, and it is a really brilliant scheme, because that means that if it is the customers who own the mortgage bank, then of course no higher contribution rates are charged than necessary. Why should you raise your own contribution rates? But when a bank and private shareholders come in as owners, suddenly it's something else, because the rates of contributions are a handle you can turn on to increase your earnings. And that's what you see. Nykredit has, of course, justified their desire to go to the stock exchange with these new capital requirements. One can then discuss whether that argument is real or not. So also for that reason, i.e. these capital requirements undermine our ability to maintain the cooperative, association-based, customer-owned, customer-controlled credit system, it is so important that we have the Danish mortgage institutions shielded against requirements that are unnecessary and that are not adapted at all to the mortgage system we have in Denmark. Because, of course, I fully agree with regulation; we are not opposed to regulation. Nor are we opposed to international agreements on the regulation of financial institutions. It is just important that it is a regulation which does not become such a one size fits all, but which actually takes into account the particularities of the individual countries. ParlaMint-DK_20160517143706	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517143727	The motion for a resolution instructs the government to set up a Commission on Freedom of expression during the current parliamentary year. The Commission must assess the conditions for freedom of expression in Denmark and propose ways of strengthening freedom of expression. The authors of the proposals have the background to the proposal, among other things. stated that the social development of recent years has meant that freedom of expression is used in public spaces under substantially different conditions than in the past. In this context, you have among other things. Reference is made to increased use of social media, increased globalisation, as well as new tools for combating terrorism, including mass monitoring. In addition, the proposers have referred to the establishment in Norway of a Commission on Freedom of Expression in the 1990s. I agree with the proposers that we should safeguard freedom of expression. It is a key element in any well-functioning democracy, and therefore the government works to ensure freedom of expression the best possible conditions in Denmark. I can already reveal that, in my view, freedom of expression has good conditions at home. This is not least due to the fact that here in the Folketing we have a long and proud tradition of discussing where the framework for example. freedom of speech must be precise. In this respect, I also welcome the motion for a resolution. Freedom of expression is, among other things, freedom of expression. protected by the Constitution, the European Convention on Human Rights and the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union. Under Paragraph 77 of the Constitution, anyone is entitled to publish his thoughts in print, in writing and in speech, but under the responsibility of the courts. Censorship and other preventive measures can never again be introduced. The provision protects all persons, all types of expressions and any form of communication. The Constitution protects formal freedom of expression, i.e.: the right to publish its statements, but on the contrary it is assumed that the Constitution does not protect material freedom of expression, i.e. freedom of expression. the right not to be subject to sanctions for certain types of utterances. Material freedom of expression is, on the other hand, among other things. protected in Article 10 of the European Convention on Human Rights and in Article 11 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union. Protection is identical according to the two provisions, and I will therefore confine my attention to Article 10 of the Convention on Human Rights. Under Article 10, everyone has the right to freedom of expression. The provision includes both the right to make utterances and the right to receive utterances. The protection provided for in Article 10 shall not be unlimited. Freedom of expression and information may be infringed if the intervention is provided for by law and is necessary in a democratic society for the sake of inter alia: national security, public security and other rights. Both the Constitution and the European Convention on Human Rights therefore lay down certain external limits for freedom of expression. When it comes to the Constitution, it is about protecting against censorship, and when it comes to the Convention on Human Rights, it is about protecting against censorship. That the Member States must act against utterances only if there is good reason to do so. It also follows that, overall, the legislative power is left quite a broad framework when it comes to defining the limits for the exercise of freedom of expression in Denmark. This framework has been used by the Folketing to lay down legislation in a number of areas restricting freedom of expression. This is true, for example. the rules laid down in Chapter 27 of the Criminal Code on violations of peace and honour and the provisions of the Administrative Act on professional secrecy. Every time this has happened, we have had a thorough debate on it in the Folketing, and so it must be. As the authors of the motion state in the explanatory statement of the proposal, the changes in society obviously also have an impact on the framework of freedom of expression, both in positive and negative terms. For example: The increase in globalisation and the spread of the Internet mean that today we can share information and thoughts with far more people than before. It is basically a positive development that enables us to influence others around the world and even to be influenced by it. But developments also present challenges. For example: It means that people abroad who want to hurt Denmark have considerably easier to spread hateful propaganda to people in this country. And this means that we need to consider whether we need to give the authorities new tools to allow them to take action against them. These considerations must take place in the Folketing. In this context, I should like to mention that the government is currently negotiating with the majority of Parliament's parties on the fight against religious preachers, and the government intends to do so, among other things. to discuss the issue of criminalising utterances. It is a difficult issue which, if implemented, will lead to a reduction in freedom of expression, which is why it is also crucial that we have a course of negotiations in which all arguments can be brought to light. If I should briefly summarise the key points in my speech, I would say that freedom of expression has basically good conditions in Denmark. It is up to the legislature to lay down the framework for freedom of expression within the scope of inter alia. the Constitution and the Convention on Human Rights. We have a long and proud tradition of discussing in the Folketing issues of enlargement and restriction of freedom of expression. This is precisely what the current negotiations on religious ministers are expressing, and the negotiations are thus also an expression of the fact that the law-making authority is ensuring that freedom of expression is taken into account in a safe manner. Against this background, it is not the government's view that there is a basis for carrying out extensive Commission work, and so it has also been said that the government cannot support the proposal.

Freedom of expression is a pillar of our democracy, and that is why we must be able to discuss it all the time if anyone finds that freedom of expression is restricted, and that is what we are doing all the time and constantly. With those words, I must say that we support freedom of expression and the debate on freedom of expression, as well as possibly work in the Criminal Law Council, but not the resolution here.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517152446	Thank you to Mrs Josephine Fock. There are no brief comments at all. Then it is Mrs Zenia Stampe, on behalf of the Radical Left.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517152455	Thank you. Freedom of speech is not exactly an under-lit area in the Danish debate. On the contrary, I can hardly think of subjects that have been surrounded by as much attention and debate as precisely freedom of expression. And it is also a very important subject which involves many different concrete and fundamental considerations. Let me just mention three examples: the debate that we had in the wake of the drawing crisis or the Mohammed crisis, I think it is a little dependent on temperament, what is called it, showed that we had a problem with violent extremists whose direct or indirect threats led to silence and self-censorship. We have also seen in the debate on the freedom of expression of public employees that there are some public authorities that take action with sanctions in a way that makes public employees self-censorship. That's a problem too. This is a problem, moreover, that we will be discussing later today, when we will be discussing a whistleblower scheme. Then we also think that it is a problem when political power begins to criminalise certain statements. Not because we have sympathy for those statements, but because we think it is contrary to a fundamental principle of a free, liberal society, namely that it must also be able to tolerate being spoken against; but also quite practically, because we think it will be harder to combat extremist positions if they are hidden in the basement instead of coming out into the light where they can be fought with open foreheads. If, however, we cannot support the proposal to set up a Commission, it is because we believe that the subject is already the subject of a lively and diverse debate. The freedom of expression and the many aspects of it, and some of the dilemmas I have mentioned, and some of the problems I have mentioned now, are already being discussed among politicians, journalists, organisations, university people, and the Ombudsman has also recently gone into the talk about the freedom of expression of public servants. So these are the players whom the proposers also imagine to be on such a commission. After all, these are already players who have so much been mobilized in the debate on freedom of expression. So what would be the purpose of such a commission and such a commission work? Yes, it would perhaps be to create the great chromed and authoritative image. And it is a beautiful thought, especially beautiful, when the debate is so diverse, so sometimes it is incalculable. However, in our best opinion, it is an illusion to believe that the result can be a number of authoritative recommendations. For as the Danish People's Party and there I have to agree, because although it may not be the paragraphs or the issues that we give priority to, I am in agreement with the approach to this debate, so I think it is a political debate. I think that there is ample explanation of where the challenges are and then it is a political decision, which instruments will be used for example. to combat radicalized imams and how much one wants to compromise on freedom of expression and so on. This is a political debate. What the Radical Left is afraid of in such a Commission is, firstly, that it is being put in the eye because we are telling each other that we can then reach the big chromed picture and agree on some recommendations. We think that is an illusion. But I am also afraid of politicising the work, that is to say that each of us brings our own little Hassan, and I really think this debate has already borne witness to this. There are the proposers and my own party who are deeply critical of the criminalisation of utterances which appear to be on the way; then there are some who are very concerned about the blasphemy clause and others may be concerned about the freedom of expression of public servants, etc. etc. That is to say, I think that before we even set up the Commission, we will be incredibly concerned about who will sit on that Commission and then draw conclusions from a discussion which is diverse, which is alive and where there are also very great differences of opinion in the Folketing. That is why I believe that this debate is best where it is now, because it is in the public debate where there are a great many people who participate with opinions, but also with explanations, both historical and legal. And then there is a political debate going on here in the Folketing about where it is that we sometimes want to compromise, or some want to compromise, where it is that the threats are considered to be greatest and so on. etc. We are happy to take part in a debate on freedom of expression. We are particularly happy to participate when it becomes concrete when it comes to the freedom of expression of public employees, which we will discuss later today. We are also happy to participate when it comes to how far the state should go in terms of criminalising utterances. We are not willing to go as far as many others are, etc. etc. However, we do not believe that a Commission work can bring out new insights, and we do not believe that a series of consensus recommendations of this kind can be reached, all of which can suddenly be endorsed after 10 years of debate, in which I believe the fronts have been drawn up fairly sharply and where it is also very clear that there are very large differences in the balance of payments. That's the way it is in a lively debate, and thank God for it. I do not think a Commission can change that, and perhaps it should not really change that either.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517155251	The debate is open and the first to speak is the Minister for Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517155312	Administrative detention under police law has been the subject of a number of questions from the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights during the spring on inter alia: the increase in the number of administrative detentions since 2005. In addition, the subject was discussed at a conciliation meeting of the Committee on Legal Affairs on 12 April. The motion for a resolution is that the government should change the rules on administrative detention. The proposal calls for clarification of the provisions of the Police Act on administrative detention of members of assemblies and meetings. The authors of the motion for a resolution want freedom of expression to be incorporated directly into the law in order to give greater weight to freedom of expression and assembly. In addition, the maximum duration of an administrative detention must be changed from 12 hours to 6 hours, which was the limit before the adoption of the so-called lumber package. As on previous occasions, I would like to make it clear at the outset that administrative detention, like the police's other tools, must, of course, be used only with regard to citizens to the extent necessary. It follows from police law that the police must work for security, security, peace and order in society. In addition, the law expressly states that the police are responsible for protecting the right of citizens to gather together. In addition, action against assemblies must, according to the law, take into account the ability of assemblies to make their views known, i.e.: the opportunity to express itself. The Police Act allows the police to intervene in certain specific circumstances against audiences and crowds. To ward off a dangerous situation, police can issue orders, carry out body inspections, or remove objects from persons. Only in cases where these interventions are not sufficient to avert the danger can the police resort to administrative detention. In all cases, the administrative detention must be necessary and the detention period must be as short as possible and as gentle as possible. The exercise of liberty against participants in assemblies and races shall, as far as possible, not exceed 12 hours. The Government considers it sensible and necessary for the police to make use of administrative detention in order to prevent and prevent danger. I understand that the authors of the motion for a resolution are basically of the opinion. However, it is a problem if the police's administrative detention is significantly subsequently overruled either by the police themselves or by the courts. As can be seen from the committee questions I answered in February on administrative detentions, there is no systematic follow-up to cases in which administrative detention is suppressed. This does not mean, however, that the police have not followed up when there have been shortcomings, as happened, for example, after the Copenhagen climate summit in 2009. Furthermore, in December 2009, a reporting system was introduced whereby all police circles were required to report administrative detention in connection with gatherings and meetings where detention has lasted more than 6 hours. As I indicated during the consultation on 12 April, on 4 April, the National Police extended the reporting system with effect from 1 January to cover all administrative detention cases, where it is subsequently assessed in either the police, the police or the courts that the conditions of the Police Act for administrative detention were not met. In replying to the questions in February and at the latest at the consultation on 12 April, I also indicated that I have asked for a work to be started to identify the situations in which the police use administrative detentions in practice. At the same time, consideration must be given to whether there is a need to make legislative adjustments or other measures in relation to the issue. The government will await this work, which is expected to be completed immediately after the summer recess, before it can be assessed whether the amendment of the law on police is to take place. In relation to the proposal to reduce the maximum length of administrative detention for participants in assemblies and meetings, it should be noted that the maximum length of administrative detentions was increased from 6 to 12 hours with the so-called tender flour package in 2009, among others. on the basis of a recommendation from the National Police Department. It is important to bear in mind that, in exceptional situations such as those in the past, it is important to take account of the fact that in exceptional circumstances such as those in the past. in the context of major events such as the Climate Summit, administrative detention may need to be extended over six hours. But, of course, detention must also be necessary in these cases and as short and gentle as possible. The National Police have indicated that they still believe that such an opportunity is needed. The government cannot therefore support the motion for a resolution as a whole.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517162333	With this resolution, the SF wants to change the rules on administrative detention, including lowering the maximum duration from 12 to 6 hours. The Minister has already examined in detail, in consultation recently, the rules, measures taken, conclusions and questions relating specifically to administrative detention. Following that review, I would like to emphasise two very important points concerning the use of administrative detention by the police. First of all, it is not an approach that the police often take. We are talking about an option that the police only use when necessary, in other words in situations where other interventions are not sufficient. Secondly, administrative detention is not a criminal offence on the part of the police. On the contrary, it is an opportunity for the police to anticipate potentially dangerous situations that could develop and pose a significant security risk. So we are talking about being at the forefront rather than at the rear end of situations that could potentially pose a risk to other people. With the two points in mind, of course, the Left is entirely in agreement with the Minister. It is both sensible and necessary for the police to be able to make use of administrative detention. As you know, work has already begun on identifying when the police are in practice using administrative detention, and consideration is also given to whether there is a need for adjustments in relation to the legislation in force in this area. We in the Left would like to await the results of this work. Because if the law is to be adjusted, and it may have to, we want to do so on an enlightened basis. In addition to clarifying the rules of the police law, proposers want to roll back the maximum duration of administrative detentions. In other words, this is the opposite of the increase made in 2009. And in 2009 the National Police recommended that the maximum duration should be raised to 12 hours because it had been found that in some cases the 6 hours were not long enough. The police have once again indicated that there are exceptional situations in which there is still a need for detention for up to 12 hours. This is an expression to which we in the Left are listening, of course, with the emphasis that administrative detention must be as short and gentle as possible at all times. It must do so with a view, among other things, to: to ensure the legal certainty of citizens. Overall, the Left cannot support this motion for a resolution, and I will tell you from the Consevatives that neither can they.

We therefore support the authors of the motion for a resolution in their desire that the law on police should be amended so that freedom of expression is directly enshrined in the law, so that it is made clear that the freedom of expression and assembly must be given more weight when the police have to balance these rights with respect to public order and security. I think it is absolutely crucial that there is no doubt that you can take part in demonstrations and that you can also stand and look at them without being at risk of being detained for administrative purposes. Developments point to the need for a priority for the sake of legal certainty in this area, in particular with regard to freedom of expression, freedom of assembly and legal certainty. Therefore, we support the clarification of sections 8 and 9 of the Police Act. I would also like to comment on the Minister's statement to look at why the use of administrative detention has then been tightened up so significantly. Of course, I'm really, really happy about that. This is among other things. on the basis of some of my own questions to the Minister that there has been an answer to that. I just think that the Minister should express concern, because the increase in the use of it suggests that something is quite wrong. There may in fact be something wrong that cannot even be resolved by means of a change in legislation. So I also look forward to having a clarification as to why the use has been so increased. But in the first place we support the SF's proposals.

In this context, I would like to make it clear that there is already an opportunity today to prosecute both the doorman concerned and, for example, a daily manager, manager or authorisation holder for violation of the Racial Discrimination Act. If a daily manager, manager or grant holder has issued oral or written guidelines that young people of ethnic origin other than Danish may not be admitted to a disco or bar, then they can already be punished. In addition, so-called corporate responsibility for violation of the Racial Discrimination Act may also be imposed. It is therefore not necessary to amend the law in order to ensure that employers may incur liability for breach of the Racial Discrimination Act. I must also understand the motion for a resolution in such a way that it is proposed to introduce what is called objective individual responsibility into the law on racial discrimination. Objective individual responsibility, in which an employer can therefore be fined for the actions of his employees, should only be introduced in exceptional circumstances. This must be seen in the context of the fact that such a form of liability cannot be attributed to the employer as intentionally or negligently that an offence has been committed. In other words, the table captures for the employer, he is liable for criminal law for the actions of his employees, although the employer cannot be blamed for anything. I sincerely believe that such a form of responsibility would be to go too far in the fight against discrimination in nightlife. In practice, such a form of liability would mean that the employer who actually has, therefore, I do not see for myself that we are introducing such objective individual responsibility. Instead, I believe that dialogue and information are the way forward. There is no need to change the law on racial discrimination, that is not the solution, so although I am absolutely convinced that the resolution has been carried by the best intentions and intentions of the proposer, the government cannot support the resolution.

SF wants to take the necessary initiatives to prevent discrimination in nightlife. This is, of course, a very important area on which the alternative has also tabled a motion for a resolution, which unfortunately did not make progress in this Chamber. What is being proposed here is to amend the Restaurant Act so that the grant boards must place emphasis on the decisions of the Equal Treatment Board in relation to the handling of grant cases and the Racial Discrimination Act, and so that the employer becomes responsible for the actions of the employees when they are at work. It is also proposed that the level of compensation for night-time discrimination should be significantly increased. In the alternative, we agree with the SF in the view that employer responsibility, which applies elsewhere, should also apply in these situations. It is not the individual doorman that we see many times today who has to determine door policy. It is the discot owner, and that is why it is also the discot owner who has to answer for it. If citizens repeatedly experience being judged out because of skin color, religion, sexuality, disability or other factors, it can lead to a sense of disregard and alienation for the rest of society. The feeling of not belonging to us is that we are married to the individual citizen as well as to society as a whole, and so we have to take these experiences very, very seriously. These are the negative experiences and feelings that we know lead to crime, abuse and ultimately radicalisation. The alternative is that we need to do much more to combat discrimination in all areas, and that is why we ourselves have made the proposal to limit discrimination in nightlife. It was a proposal in which it was recommended that a package be included in the grant application, so to speak, where human rights were brought to the attention of the discotists, and in this way they were made further aware that they have a very special responsibility for letting people in. As I said, this proposal did not obtain a majority, but it should not prevent us from supporting this. We also agree that regulation combined with work on attitudes through campaigns can be a solution, and hopefully that combination will also be effective in the field of discrimination in nightlife. We therefore support the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517173330	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517173358	Thank you very much. The motion for a resolution instructs the government to establish a whistleblower scheme for public employees before 1 January. The Whistleblower scheme shall be designed as an independent body, including: be able to receive anonymous reports from public servants wishing to draw attention to illegal management or serious maladministration. As part of the scheme, investigations must also be possible on the basis of the reports and, if the studies provide a basis for this, criticism must be made of the authorities. In addition, the proposal instructs the government to introduce a number of protection measures for whistleblowers. This concerns, among other things: to increase the protection of employment in the form of rules on shared burden of proof and increased compensation in cases of dismissal. The authors of the proposals have the background to the proposal, among other things. stated that democracy and openness are fundamental values in the public sector and that Denmark is better off than many other countries in terms of transparency and corruption. In the case of public servants who wish to reveal, for example: However, the authors of the proposals point out that the situation was supposed to be flawed. This is partly due to the fact that, according to the proposers, public servants are all too often left to raise cases in the press, which is inappropriate, both for the whistleblowers and the authorities. I agree with the proposers that we should safeguard the freedom of expression of public employees. It is essential for a well-functioning democracy that public servants share their knowledge in the public debate. It hangs, among other things. in conjunction with the involvement of public servants in raising the professional level. For example, public employees can provide information on the practical consequences of a given political decision or are likely to have. That is why I have recently called on all ministries, the National Association of Municipalities and the Danish Regions to contribute with a strengthened awareness effort on the freedom of expression of public employees. Efforts must ensure that public servants know the extent of their rights, so that they are confident that rights are also respected in practice. At the same time, I have called on the ministries, the National Association of Municipalities and the Danish Regions to ensure that the authorities in their areas consider whether it is appropriate to establish a whistleblower scheme. I have done so in the context of the follow-up to report No. 1553 of the Committee on the Freedom of expression and whistleblower schemes of public employees, which came in April last year. The Committee was established in 2013 by the then government. The Committee has held a Supreme Court judge as chairman and has also been widely composed with the participation of, inter alia: Professors from universities and representatives from the media and staff organisations. The Committee has had the task of assessing whether there is a need for enhanced information and advice, legislation on the freedom of expression of public employees, increased employment rights for public employees who have used their freedom of expression, and the establishment of a special board to ensure the freedom of expression of public employees. The Committee has also had to highlight the advantages and disadvantages of whistleblower schemes in the public administration and assess the need for such a scheme. A report was submitted last April, and I shall not go into it in detail, but will focus on those parts that are most relevant to the resolution. They are whistleblower schemes and shared burden of proof rules, they are burden of proof and increased remuneration. As regards whistleblower schemes, a unanimous committee considered that general legislation should not be implemented. The Committee emphasized that consideration should be given both to the establishment of such a system and to the establishment of such a system. As an argument in favour, among other things, that it can increase the basis for democratic debate and control, that it can help to raise the level of government services and that it can strengthen the credibility of the authorities. As an argument against the introduction of whistleblower schemes, it is argued, among other things, that it may result in fewer people calling attention to maladministration by name and that it may be difficult to assess the credibility of anonymous notifications. The Committee also points out that whistleblower schemes can create a poor working environment because employees are afraid of being hung up and that this may involve certain legal concerns with regard to persons who are examined on the basis of anonymous notifications. The Committee also considered that the need for whistleblower arrangements and their appropriateness vary from authority to authority, both in relation to the size of the authority, the type of tasks and the existing control structure. Against this background, the Committee recommended that consideration be given to the appropriateness of a whistleblower scheme within each authority and this recommendation by a unanimous committee has been listened to by the government. I would now like to turn to the committee's recommendations for increased employment protection, specifically the rules on shared burden of proof and increased compensation. The rules on shared burden of proof would roughly imply that the responsibility for proving whether an employee has been fired due to his legitimate statements may shift from the employee to the employer in a court case. The rules on increased compensation will mean that the employer will have to pay more indemnification to the employee who will be fired unjustly because of the legal expression. The committee did not agree on the need for new rules in this area, with six Members voting in favour, five votes against and six abstentions. The government has chosen to follow the recommendation of the five members who voted against from among them the chairman of the committee. They pointed out that the burden of proof in this type of legal proceedings is already divided in practice. The general rules thus imply that the burden of proof will change to the employer if there is reason to believe that an employee has been fired because of his legitimate statements. The government therefore sees no need to introduce new rules on the subject. Furthermore, the five members have pointed out that rules on increased compensation will not significantly lead public employees to make more use of their freedom of expression. This is due, among other things, to the fact that public employees are typically reluctant to comment on conditions within their workplace because they are afraid of creating a poor working environment or of having career consequences, for example. that they are frozen out. Rules on increased compensation will not change this fear. If I should briefly summarise the key points of my speech, the question of whistleblower schemes and increased right of employment protection has been carefully considered by a wide-ranging committee. The Government agrees with the Committee's unanimous recommendation that no general legislation on a whistleblower scheme should be introduced, but that the need to establish such a scheme should be considered in concrete terms within each authority. The Government agrees with the part of the committee which has recommended that no rules on shared burden of proof and increased remuneration should be introduced, since such rules cannot be assumed to encourage public employees to make greater use of their freedom of expression. That is why it is not surprising that the government cannot support the proposal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517183137	Thank you to the SF rapporteur. Then I give the floor to the rapporteur for the proposers, Mrs Josephine Fock, the alternative.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517183156	Thank you. I would like to begin by thanking the Enhedslisten, the Liberal Alliance, the Radical Left and the SF for supporting the intentions of the proposal, and then I will come back to the specific comments made by the parties concerned. We have made this proposal because we believe that whistleblowers are an important feature of modern democracy. This is true both in less serious cases and in very large cases. In fact, in the alternative, we consider whistleblowers to be not only desirable, but to be a democratic necessity for our society. If I may mention a few of the great examples, we have Edward Snowden, who proved to the whole world how the American surveillance programmes were monitoring illegally to an extent that none of us had the imagination to imagine. Then we have seen it with the Danish intelligence officer, Anders Kærgaard, who, after a long struggle to deal with the matter internally, chose to go to the press with his knowledge that Danish forces were watching detained Iraqis being abused by Iraqi forces without being able to intervene. Most recently we have seen it with Luxleaks, where the former employee of PriceWaterhouseCoopers, Antoine Deltour, revealed the extent of the extensive tax evasion in Luxembourg. These are all matters which have a considerable interest in the public and in the view of the alternative also for democracy. We therefore believe that whistleblowers should be recognised for the important efforts they make for democracy and that they should be offered orderly conditions to report their knowledge. This applies, whether it is a major political illegality such as those I have just mentioned, they reveal, or whether it is in a more common everyday context. Of course, if this is illegal or to the detriment of the principles of the administration, we must be able to draw attention to it so that we can rectify it. Here comes the second important point of our motion for a resolution: as I said, it is by no means just about the big issues. On the contrary, it is very much about the problems of everyday life. It is a question of employees who suspect or know of criminal offences and other serious conditions at their place of work being able to allow a professional body to handle the matter. And there is certainly a demand for such a body. The City of Copenhagen is among those who take the lead in this question. Here you have established your own whistleblower scheme with the Citizens' Advisor, who is called the Ombudsman on a daily basis. During the last 31⁄2 years of operation of the scheme, it has received more than five monthly reports, two thirds of which have been assessed to be within the scope of the scheme. And most importantly, it can be said, it is that in almost half of the cases the Citizen's Adviser confirmed that the reported information was either wholly or partly correct. Note the figures they are really large, and this shows that there is a need to set up such schemes. It is important for me to stress that we are not talking about matters where the colleague uses the copier for a private purpose. We are talking about offences or other serious negligence, whether intentionally or unconsciously. The main thing is that we need to detect errors and that we need to be able to correct them. And the experience in the City of Copenhagen shows just a need to go somewhere if you discover mistakes. But the real problem is in fact all the examples that I cannot give here on the platform. I am talking about cases where illegal or gross negligence has been committed in the administration, but which we never hear about because no one is standing up and drawing attention to them. There may be many reasons why you, as a public servant, are not standing up, anonymously or not, but one thing that is clear to me is that it should never be a lack of places to go to blow the whistle, which is why public employees do not get their knowledge reported. We therefore believe, in the alternative, that it is the task of the State to ensure that the necessary opportunities are available. In other words, it is the state that is to facilitate the whistleblower. It's not the whistleblower who's supposed to facilitate the state. And one way to ensure that public employees have the opportunity to blow the whistle is to create a whistleblower scheme to which all public employees can turn. That is what we are proposing with this motion for a resolution. We should therefore ensure as soon as possible that public servants who are aware of any unlawfulness, maladministration or maladministration of a certain degree of seriousness can properly report it. It also means that they need not fear for their careers or, as we have seen in the most serious cases, fear to be exposed to a personal hetz. And that requires trustworthy, professional and anonymous access to report on these cases. That is the real reason why we have proposed one central system. I shall then hear the comments that are made in relation, for example, to the question of whether we are going to be able to do something about it. could create several schemes, one for the municipalities, one for the regions and one for the state, or in some other way divide it up, and we are completely open to that. The reason why we have proposed one body and we have also had thoughts about whether that body should be under the authority of the Ombudsman, as the Radical Left mentioned, is precisely that we want to ensure that there is proper professionalism about it. For it is of course important that there is legal certainty, both for the person reporting it, but also very much for the person who may be reported for having made a mistake. It is really crucial for us that there is a professional, sound security in a whistleblower system. We see a wide range of advantages in creating a whistleblower system. Firstly, it will mean that whistleblowers need not fear personal or career consequences because cases are handled confidentially or anonymously and as far as possible within the organisation. With regard to the comments made here from the platform on the consequences of employment law, I can say that we have taken it with us because a united trade union movement, it can be said, both LO, FTF and AC, wants such a right of employment security. Those who are needed think so. But the alternative is certainly willing to discuss how and how such a system should be combined in relation to the consequences of employment law. Secondly, a whistleblower system will ensure that we take the whistleblower out of the equation, if you may say so. We maintain the focus on the content of the case rather than on the person. In other words, we ensure that you always go for the ball, the case, instead of the man. Thirdly, it will strengthen the credibility of the authorities on the outside that there is a body that independently and impartially deals with reports of unlawfulness and serious maladministration, rather than that a case is never allowed to see the light of day or that it ends up in the press. There is a risk that an entire authority will soon be taken to account for perhaps the wrong actions of a single employee, which I assume none of us in this Chamber considers appropriate. Then it has been said several times during the debate that in report 1553 on the freedom of expression of public employees and whistleblower schemes it is not recommended to introduce compulsory whistleblower schemes in all authorities. Instead, the committee behind the report recommends that all authorities be invited to consider whether it is appropriate to introduce a system for themselves. And recently, the Minister of Justice has issued a shepherd letter following the report to all ministries, KL and Danske Regions. Here, the recipients of the letter are asked to draw their respective organisations' attention to the recommendation that consideration should be given to the need to establish a whistleblower scheme within each organisation. I am, of course, pleased that the Minister has taken the initiative in this regard, and I would like to acknowledge that. At the same time, however, I must acknowledge that this is not enough for the alternative. There are two problems. Firstly, a year has now passed since the report was published, and that should have been more than enough time for the various authorities to consider whether to introduce a whistleblower system. Nevertheless, few municipalities and regions have started to follow up the call. Thus we could all read in Politiken on Sunday that not a single ministry or their underlying agencies has introduced a whistleblower scheme. Nor does it appear that there are any arrangements under way. In any case, according to the article in Politiken, the Danish Data Protection Agency, which has to approve all whistleblower schemes, states that no agencies or ministries have applied to establish a whistleblower scheme. Secondly, there is the problem that, although in the future we may see some ministries and government departments and other public authorities in municipalities and regions setting up whistleblower schemes, there will in all likelihood still be a number of authorities which do not. This is a problem, for who should say whether there has been any illegal activity in a particular authority? In particular in the more heavy authorities such as the defence, the Ministry of Justice and the like, it is necessary that employees have a place to go if they become aware of unlawful or gross negligence. Whether it should be a central board in line with what we are proposing or whether it should be a local board, we are, as I said, fully willing to discuss. But I think it is really important that we get started now. That is also why we have tabled the proposal on whistleblower schemes. As I said, I am very pleased with the comments made by the Enhedslisten, the Liberal Alliance, the Radical Left and the SF that you are backing up. And I am quite willing to look at the problems that you have each mentioned in a future committee. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517184214	The debate is open and I give the floor to the Minister for Justice.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517184234	The motion for a resolution instructs the government to take the necessary initiatives to follow the proposal's 16 recommendations on data security in total. The recommendations are the same as those made by the Committee on Legal Affairs in the January 2015 report on data security. We in the government have dealt with the report in the reply of the Ministry of Justice of 14 January this year to the Legal Affairs Committee's question No. 147. Let me start by stating that data protection is an area that both the government and the Committee on Legal Affairs take very seriously and want to give priority to it. The SE and Hør case in spring 2014 and the case of the hacker attack in 2012 on the CSC and the National Police are two sad examples of the fact that our information was not well taken care of. For me, as Minister of Justice, it is crucial that we do everything we can to avoid cases such as these. That is also why the government is on the way with a number of initiatives in the area of data protection, and it is worth noting initiatives that will take into account the recommendations and underlying concerns of the resolution. There is a good reason why we should come up with these initiatives in the area of data protection. Private companies and public authorities are increasingly processing information about citizens, a trend that only grows in line with the increase in digitalisation in society. Denmark is a digital pioneer country, and we must remain so, but as citizens we must be able to rely on our personal data to be processed safely and legally. This is true both when we leave our personal data to companies and authorities and when they collect them themselves. That is why, as I said, a number of initiatives have been taken in the area of data protection. Following the SE and HØR case, the former government set up an inter-ministerial working group to map the protection of information on citizens' electronic payments, etc. Before the summer holidays, the working group will make a number of recommendations which I look forward to receiving. The survey will be the starting point for a broad political discussion on data security, to which I look forward very much. In the light of these political discussions, the Government will draw up an overall strategy to ensure the personal data of the Danes. In addition, as you know, the EU's new General Data Protection Regulation was adopted last month and the Regulation will apply from 25 May 2018. We in Denmark have actively contributed to the negotiations, and my officials have continuously had contact with many different actors in the field in order to get the flag the special Danish interests in the best possible way. Overall, the government is satisfied with the outcome of the negotiations, although it is difficult to balance the specific interests of 28 countries. A subject which is often highlighted in relation to the future regulation has been the question of the amount of fines. The subject has also received considerable attention from the Danish side. There is no doubt that with the significant fines in the future regulation, there will be increased focus on compliance with rules and data protection as such. It will require very thorough preparatory work to translate the Data Protection Regulation into a Danish context, and I have therefore asked the officials to launch a comprehensive in-service project work on the new data protection regulation. We have posted a project description on the Ministry of Justice's website about the work. In the first quarter of 2017, the work will lead to a publication of analyses, recommendations and draft legislative adjustments resulting from the regulation. In the forthcoming analysis, the recommendations made by the Committee on Legal Affairs in January 2015 will also be included. In connection with the work there are many questions that need to be answered: What should happen to the Personal Data Act? What will happen to special legislation? What is involved in the role of data protection officer etc. etc? Subsequently, the legislative proposals required by the Data Protection Regulation will be submitted to the Danish Parliament in autumn 2017. I would like to emphasise that we want all stakeholders in this area to be involved in the work. We would also like to inform the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights of the progress of work on the regulation on a regular basis during the forthcoming political discussions. I therefore look forward to the forthcoming political discussions and hope that everyone will take constructively the initiatives mentioned. In conclusion, may I highlight the good work done by the Data Protection Agency. This is a major and difficult task that supervision promises. That is why I decided earlier this year that, in cooperation with the Data Protection Agency, we should look at the current tasks and resources of the supervisory authority in order to add more money to the Data Protection Agency. As a result, the government is planning to strengthen the Data Protection Agency by approx. 2 million kr. Annually from 2017 onwards, which corresponds to an increase in appropriations of approx. 10% In addition, the question of supervisory resources will be considered on an ongoing basis in the context of the work on the Data Protection Regulation. In the light of the initiatives mentioned and thus already planned, the government cannot therefore support the resolution.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517191911	This proposal is to be understood as a reminder that on 15 January last year it was a unanimous Committee on Legal Affairs which presented a report on how we in Denmark can strengthen data security. Because, as several of the other rapporteurs in this Chamber have said, society is becoming increasingly digitalised; more and more digital solutions are coming; we need to serve ourselves digitally when we turn to the municipality; and we all get smartphones with all sorts of apps. We are seeing an ever-increasing trend towards a digitalised society, which also offers many opportunities to us as people and as citizens of society. But there's also a security aspect in this digitalization, which needs to be taken care of very well. And it was the case that in the report presented by the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights almost a year and a half ago, there were a number of very good concrete proposals on how we can improve data security here in Denmark. And because time has just passed and because there has been a little silence on the part of the government, this resolution has been tabled where we have taken the 16 most concrete recommendations from the report and they have been incorporated into this resolution. And it's to put some turbo on the process to ensure that when a report is made in a parliamentary committee, even with all parties behind it, it doesn't just get forgotten or fall between two chairs or be downgraded in relation to other agendas. So this is a mover; it's a reminder that something is about to happen. I am pleased to note today that, unless the government comes up with concrete initiatives, a concrete timetable, some concrete proposals on how we can push this process forward in order to follow up on the report of the Committee on Legal Affairs about a year and a half ago, there will be a majority here in the Danish Parliament who will send a friendly reminder to the government and say: now something is going to happen in this very important area, which is precisely about data security. There are 16 specific proposals. Proposal No 1 is that an annual report on data security is to be prepared for the Folketing. Proposal No 2 is that an instance will be set up that follows up data security breaches and draws conclusions on the basis of the breach and that will make the experience available to other authorities and businesses. This is what we call an IT Commission in everyday IT language, which will have to collect experience and also find out who is responsible if there has been a breach of data security, in the same way as we have an accident commission, which will deal with a situation if, for example, there is one. is a bridge that may have collapsed somewhere in Denmark. Proposal No 3 is that more resources should be allocated to the Data Protection Agency, and the Minister indicated that he would like to add 2 million. kr. extra. It's a step on the way, you might say. If Enhedslisten had to decide, our bid would be, as we have put it in our IT proposal, which we presented almost two years ago, that the grant to the Data Protection Agency should be adjusted upwards by 50%, and that would actually mean that 10 million would be added. kr. But okay, it's a step along the way, and it's nice to get that statement from the government that you put a concrete amount of money on what you've been thinking about strengthening the Data Protection Agency. Then is Recommendation No. 4 that we are looking at how the Danish Data Protection Agency can be linked closer to the Danish Parliament. And it comes from the fact, as the report by the Committee on Legal Affairs has also shown, that the Council on Digital Security has stated that it supports the location of a data protection authority in the Folketing, because the Council does not believe that the Data Protection Authority, with its current position under the Ministry of Justice, fulfils the independence which, for example, exists. is laid down in the Charter of Fundamental Human Rights of the European Union. And that is simply what forms the basis of Recommendation No. 4. Recommendation No 5, as some of the rapporteurs have also mentioned, is about sanctions • Why should it be the case, in the context of a breach of data security, that private companies can be punished for some offences, while the public authorities cannot be punished for the same offences? In other words, this is a way to ensure that the same rules apply to both private and public. And that must be important here, because as a citizen of this country, you can, so to speak, be indifferent to where the breach is going, if it is the same break-up, for example. Lying and flowing information all sorts of places, very personal information. Then there are various other proposals which are also included in the 16 proposals I cannot go through all of them. However, I would like to mention Amendment No 1. 9 and 10, which is of course about privacy by design, so that we have to set up our IT systems in such a way that if there is a data leak, if there is suddenly information about citizens in society, then that information is made in such a way that you cannot deduce who the information is about. And that is something like that, for example. do not use the cpr number as a key that you connect other information to, but give people a user number that is unique to the database where the information is posted. In any case, the systems must be designed in such a way that a break or data leak occurs, it cannot be deduced who the sensitive information actually relates to. There is also another recommendation that I would like to bring forward, which is about control of user access, in other words that it should be registered as a requirement in public tender contacts. And if I may highlight one of the points in the long 23-page answer given by the Minister for Justice, following on from the report, which was given almost a year and a half ago, this is one of the few places where the government actually indicates that there is some sense in it and that this is something you want to look at. So there is at least one place where you go in and take a positive view of the report, and that is a good thing. Then there are a lot of other proposals too, but the last thing I want to mention is Amendment No. 16, which is of course about the fact that the use of the CPR number should undergo a basic revision, including that the surrender of the CPR number should not only be able to constitute authentication. And after all, the cpr number is also about the data leak from the CSC, which several have mentioned from up here, where 900,000 cpr numbers were available for just under an hour's time because of the data breach that was • not safe, as it is today. And it is quite clear that it must somehow be replaced by a new system or at least be of less importance. It certainly cannot be a crank in the way it has been before, because the system is simply too untimely it is not modern and it is too uncertain. But I note today that unless something very concrete comes from the Minister for Justice, a majority in the Danish Parliament will send a reminder to the government to say that when a unanimous Committee on Legal Affairs in the Danish Parliament says that we want to improve data security, we are actually serious about this. And I therefore hope that the Minister will provide a clear timetable and a clear indication of what is going to happen and some very concrete answers to the very concrete measures contained in the report, and not something that I consider to be too loose, which does not respond directly to the recommendations and concrete initiatives that are actually contained in the report. And that's the answer to question No. 147 which I am referring to here and which I have mentioned a few times. There we must therefore have a detailed examination, in which we simply look, for each specific proposal contained in the report, at what the government has done and what the government intends to do to ensure precisely the purpose of the report, namely to significantly increase data security for the Danes. I also believe that in this context we can use the new IT forum, as Mr. René Gade has taken the initiative here in the Danish Parliament, which consists of the IT rapporteurs, and in which we can, of course, also discuss it in great detail and follow the concrete proposals contained in the report, where 16 have been taken out here. For the 16 points which have been included in this motion for a resolution are, after all, all very important points, and each of them could, in fact, be changed in order to have a proper discussion of it. So there is a lot of work to be done to follow up on the report and what lies there. But I am certainly glad that unless the government moves out with something more and some more initiatives, there is a majority in the Folketing here who will send the government a reminder.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517193210	Thank you, President. I thank Enhedslisten for the proposal to change the offsetting of municipal use of operating income from municipal renewable energy installations. The Enhedslisten explains the proposal that the set-off rules in the Electricity Act are not adapted so that they are up-to-date in relation to investments in renewable energy installations and the participation of municipalities in this area. The Enhedslisten believes that the profits from renewable energy installations should not be used only within the separate company, but should also be able to be used for other climate improvement measures such as: communal energy saving measures. It is the Government's ambition in the field of energy that Denmark should become independent of fossil fuels by 2050. With the 2012 Energy Agreement, which was concluded by a broad circle of parties, Denmark has moved some way towards independence from fossil fuels. However, the Government cannot support Enhedslisten's proposal as it will allow municipalities to use the profits of existing electricity generating companies for other earmarked activities. If municipalities wish to finance climate improvement initiatives, this should be done as part of a general priority for the tax-financed municipal funds. In addition, the government considers that a relaxation of the set-off rules should not be used to promote municipal investments in electricity generation from renewable energy sources in competition with private companies. In the current rules, it is already possible for municipalities to run electricity supply activities with renewable energy installations. However, this must be done in a separate and separated company and the municipalities cannot draw a profit from the company and use it for normal operation without offsetting the block grant. The current rules therefore already allow municipalities to have renewable energy installations. That is why the government rejects the Enhedslisten's proposal.

We think it would be unfair competition if municipalities could go into business. This is a field in which we must allow private companies to operate, and public authorities must stay out of it. In addition, there is the view that when the public authorities have to finance their services to the public, they have to do so with the greatest possible transparency. And it is by levying taxes on which everyone can see the rates, and so everyone can see what they pay and not by doing business at the same time, which also provides a return that goes into the municipal economy. So we also reject the motion for a resolution because we do not want the opaqueness that this could lead to.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517195003	Thank you for that, President. When considering the motion for a resolution B 95 on the modification of the offsetting of municipal use of operating income from municipal renewable energy installations, the Left votes against the proposal. With the resolution, the Enhedslisten refers to relaxing the principle of offsetting, and of course the Left is against this, as it is not appropriate to allow municipalities to use any surplus for activities earmarked for other purposes. Funding for climate prevention initiatives should continue to be given priority to municipal tax-financed funds and, if the principle of offsetting is relaxed, local authorities' motivation to invest in renewable energy installations is increased. These plants will compete directly with private providers of renewable energy installations, and we are simply not interested in that in the Left. We must have fair relations for all parties. Current legislation indicates that it is already possible for municipalities to have renewable energy installations as long as their profits remain within the municipal utility company. But, of course, the municipalities must not be able to use their profits for other purposes without offsetting the block subsidy. That is why the Left is convinced that the principle of set-off ensures equal conditions for all, and we are not inclined to change that. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517200934	Thank you, President. As I have emphasised many times, the Paris Agreement is a historic turning point for the global transition to low-mission, and it sets the framework for our future international climate cooperation. With the agreement, all the countries of the world are committed to drawing up national climate plans to reduce their emissions. 189 countries have already presented their plans, which cover more than 95%. of global emissions.
