We worked purposefully for an ambitious climate agreement and managed to secure a number of our priorities. I'm proud of that. Just four weeks ago, I myself had the pleasure of signing the agreement on behalf of Denmark at the United Nations headquarters in New York with 174 Heads of State and Government and other ministers. With our presence, we marked our strong support for the agreement and the work to implement it. The Paris Agreement must now be ratified by both the EU Member States and the EU as a whole. The government has always stated clearly that we will ratify the Paris Agreement as soon as possible and that under no circumstances will Denmark delay the EU's overall ratification. There's nothing new about that. That is why I am not going to accept the alternative resolution either. I see no reason why a resolution should be tabled requiring the government to ratify as soon as possible. This is, of course, a point on which there is consensus across the board. The government has even presented a clear timetable for Danish ratification. Last Thursday, I announced that the government will, as soon as possible in the next parliamentary assembly, table a motion for a resolution on Denmark's ratification of the Paris Agreement. As I said, the Government supports the Paris Agreement and stresses that it can enter into force as soon as possible. That is why I hope that by presenting a concrete timeframe, we can also help to push for the EU and its Member States to ratify the agreement as soon as possible. We must remember that Denmark can first and foremost become a party to the agreement once the other Member States and the EU as a whole have ratified the agreement. This also means, of course, that although we have completed our national ratification process, we still have to wait for the rest of the European Union to implement theirs before we can become parties to the agreement. So far, France, as far as I know, is the only country in the European Union that has reported clearly when it wants to ratify. I will therefore also work to ensure that the European Union announces a clear timetable for the ratification of the Paris Agreement by the European Union. ParlaMint-DK_20160517204529	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister of Energy, Supply and Climate, please.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517204551	Thank you, Mr President. And thank you for the proposal from the Radical Left. With their proposals, the Radical Left wishes to promote the installation of large heat pumps and to limit the expansion of biomass boilers. According to the proposal, by 1 November of this year the government is to submit a draft law on the promotion of heat pumps. It is also clear from the proposal that the Folketing attaches importance to ensuring that the green conversion takes place as efficiently as possible. I am pleased that we basically agree that we must carry out the green transition economically effectively. It is the Government's ambition in the field of energy that Denmark should become independent of fossil fuels by 2050. With the 2012 Energy Agreement, which was concluded by a broad circle of parties, Denmark has come some way towards independence from fossil fuels. At the same time, it is an important premise for the government that the green transition is precisely cost-effective. That is what we in the government call green realism. The conversion must not be unnecessarily expensive for business and ordinary citizens. The government wants to integrate renewable electricity production into district heating. It is a way to exploit the increasing share of the renewable current in the Danish electricity system. Increased electricity in the district heating sector can have a major impact on cost-effective energy conversion. It's a natural part of a smart energy system. The Government therefore supports the spread of heat pumps. With the approval of the conciliation circle in connection with the implementation of the energy reserve in December 2015, funds were allocated to the Danish Energy Agency's travel team for large heat pumps. The travel team is working to promote large electric heat pumps. One of the most important tasks of the travel team is to help the heating plants clarify whether heat pumps can help achieve a lower heat price. The government has put forward a proposal for a solution to our PESO challenge by moving over a four-year period the funding of the PSO support away from the electricity price and into the Finance Act. This proposal will in itself make it more profitable to use large heat pumps in the heat supply. It is the assessment that when the PSO cost is removed, the heat pumps are strengthened in competition in competition with a chips-fired heating plant. The discussions on the energy savings obligations of the energy companies have also put forward a proposal that energy saving by using a heat pump can be counted on. That is why I believe that heat pumps can be a real alternative for the many cogeneration plants that will have to invest in the coming years. The Government has already presented concrete initiatives to promote heat pumps. The proposal we are debating today also concerns the use of biomass. Let me make it clear that biomass plays a crucial role in some of the smallest decentralised district heating plants. Thus, a decision has been taken in the broad circle of parties behind the energy conciliation to allow the 85 most expensive heating plants to set up a biomass boiler. This gives the works an opportunity to lower the high heat prices. At the same time, it provides a limited expansion of biomass combustion, as only 85 named smaller plants have been authorised. This is not a massive conversion wave, but an aid to the heat consumers at the highest prices. There was also agreement in the energy conciliation circle to promote greater biomass conversion in the large cogeneration plants. Many central plants therefore replace coal with biomass in these years, and this will be a sustainable biomass, as an industry agreement has been established that provides the most ambitious sustainability requirements in the European Union. Surely it is only good that we are thus paving the way for an energy system that is independent of fossil fuels. The government is carrying out a number of analyses of the future framework for the district heating sector. One of the key analyses is the tax and subsidy analysis, which will help shed light on the need for adjustments in this area. The analysis takes time, even more time than expected, when the work was started by the former government. The government focuses on the analysis, but we will not rush it, as it is important that the analysis is thorough and complete. In addition, there are analyses that look more specifically at the future framework conditions for the district heating sector, including opportunities to promote the use of large heat pumps and the framework for the use of biomass. Some key issues that need to be clarified before we start to change the system are: Is there a need to adjust the charges, even after the government's new spring proposals? What possible models are there to loosen on the bindings that cogeneration plants are subject to today in relation to cogeneration requirements and fuels? And what are the consequences for heat consumers? In short, these are very wide-ranging and complex issues. I agree with the Radical Left that it is a good idea to promote the use of heat pumps for district heating. As has already been said, the government has already put forward concrete proposals to promote large heat pumps, and so the motion for a resolution is superfluous on this point. Nor can the government support the decision on the future use of biomass on an imperfect basis, and therefore cannot support the resolution on this point either. On the other hand, the government is looking forward to future constructive discussions in this area, including with the Radical Left.

ParlaMint-DK_20160517205806	Thank you, President. First of all, I would like to acknowledge the issue raised by the Radical Left with the motion for a resolution here. ParlaMint-DK_20160517212333	Thank you very much. Greenland's sea area is 2.1 million. mile2 big. To establish charts in Greenland is therefore a big, but also a necessary task. ParlaMint-DK_20160517212635	Thank you to the rapporteur. Then I give the floor to the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate for reply.
ParlaMint-DK_20160517212654	Thank you for that, President. I would also like to thank you for the question which I will try to answer in the best possible way in the following. As Members of this House will probably know, the government wants state jobs to be distributed throughout the country. Therefore, the government moves an historically large number of state jobs from the capital to the four other regions of the country. The move is being carried out in order to achieve better balance and development across the country and to ensure state institutions close to citizens and businesses. One of the agencies to move is the Spatial Data Board, which from 1 November 2016 will move to Aalborg. One consequence of this move is unfortunately that the cooperation agreement with Greenland's Self-Government on the restoration and digitisation of charts covering Greenland's south-west coast is delayed. The Co-operation Agreement, concluded between the Ministry of the Environment and self-government in 2009, provides that charts of South West Greenland shall be established, restored and digitised by the end of 2018. Specifically, the agreement concerns a total of 73 charts, of which the Geodata Board has currently produced 32. The Cooperation Agreement is an ambitious and important agreement for Greenland's autonomy and the Danish Government. It is important for me to make it clear that the cooperation agreement only deals with sea charting and not sea surveying. The concept of chart survey has been mentioned several times in this debate, but it is not a correct concept because in the maritime world a sharp distinction is made between sea surveying and charting. Sea surveying is carried out in order to gain knowledge of depth conditions over a sea area. The survey, which is the basis for the charting of the sea, is normally carried out from a ship which collects all information above the depth conditions of a wide belt of the seabed. The chart reproduces all information relevant to navigation in a paper, chart, including the results of the sea survey. The chart will also be published digitally. When the charts are digitized, they can be displayed on electronic navigation systems, and new corrections and information can be implemented easily and easily. The restoration involves digitising and positioning the old charts so that they can be used together with GPS, navigation and new sea surveying. Greenland's sea area is approx. 20 times the sea area of Denmark. It is therefore not possible to cover such an enormous sea area with modern charts at once. It passes approx. 60,000 larger ships pass through Danish waters every year, and by comparison approx. 600 large ships through Greenland waters every year. The cooperation agreement with Greenland's Self-Government is precisely about the restoration and digitisation of the charts of the sea area, which can be sailed half of the year or more, where the majority of all shipping traffic takes place and where the majority of the population lives. The priority of which charts are to be restored first has been achieved in cooperation with the self-government. The Spatial Data Board has published, restored and digitised charts in most of the coastal area that can normally be sailed throughout the year. 32 of the planned 73 charts have been produced, which means that the Spatial Data Board has to produce 41 charts by the end of 2018 in order to comply with the cooperation agreement with Greenland's self-government. In this context, it is important to stress that charts already exist throughout the area covered by the cooperation agreement. However, these charts were produced at a time when there were no good models for mapping the northern latitudes. The old charts can be used for navigation with traditional navigation techniques, but they cannot be used for satellite navigation with GPS, as they are not positioned correctly in relation to the GPS system. Until the charts of these areas are produced, restored and digitized, it is therefore necessary to use the old charts and traditional navigation techniques for sailing. The sea survey of Greenland waters is expected to continue to the same extent as before the relocation of the Spatial Data Board. The same applies to the publication of vital sailing information of importance to shipping in Greenland, which is also not expected to be affected by relocation. Production of charts in Denmark takes place exclusively in the Spatial Data Board. It is a complex work that requires very specific competences, and it takes a long time to train new workers, as this is primarily done by side-by-side training. The Spatial Data Board is working on high pressure to minimise the consequences of the relocation of the production of the new Greenlandic charts, among other things. by continuously hiring new employees and obtaining assistance from the Danish Agency's international sister organisations and maritime partners. But it is the board's assessment that it will be a few years before the new employees can fully manage the production of charts for Greenland, and it is unfortunately inevitable that production will be delayed. The delay is thus not due to a lack of financial resources, but to a large loss of knowledge in connection with the relocation of the Spatial Data Board to Aalborg. Out of 15 employees working with Greenlandic charts in the Spatial Data Board, 12 have currently left their position, and another 2 employees have announced that they do not want to follow Aalborg. At the annual contact committee meeting with representatives from Greenland's Self-Government in December 2015, the Spatial Data Board informed that the proposed relocation to Aalborg could risk influencing the progress of chart production to an extent that could have an impact on the implementation of the cooperation agreement. The self-government was finally informed of the delay at the beginning of April of this year, just as I informed my Greenlandic colleague Knud Kristiansen about the situation on 5 April. It is a great exercise to implement the largest and most ambitious plan of Danish history for the relocation of state jobs, and this will unfortunately affect the operational situation in a number of the governments concerned. We have a strong focus on implementing the relocation of state jobs as efficiently and flexibly as possible. There has been discussion of compensatory measures and possible future deliveries with self-government, and the close dialogue with self-government on any updating of the cooperation agreement continues. It is therefore still too early to say how much production is being delayed, as it is, among other things, too late. depends on the staff situation and ongoing discussions with the self-government on the revision of the Cooperation Agreement. I shall be the first to regret this unfortunate situation and delay, but now it is important to look ahead and find a solution with which Greenland's autonomy and government can be served. I would therefore suggest that the Spatial Data Board and the Greenlandic selfgovernment should be allowed to draw up a revised cooperation agreement which takes into account the implications of the government's plan for the relocation of state jobs and takes the best possible account of the parties involved. Thank you for the floor.

I take the situation quite seriously, and I would also like to say in reply to some of the questions that have been put, that it is absolutely essential for the government that we do everything possible to resolve this situation. It is clear that 14 out of 15 employees with a very special knowledge of chart surveying have chosen to terminate their jobs in connection with the transfer of the Spatial Data Board to Aalborg, of course, is a challenge. Therefore, we are also doing what we can to deal with this as well as possible. But it is also clear that there is a need to make a new agreement with Greenland's Self-Government. It is not possible to produce the charts agreed by the deadline of 2018. This is also why it has been crucial for the government that in this process we have had good contact with Greenland and have discussed this with the Greenlandic politicians, so that everyone has been aware of what this situation is that we are in. I would like to say that both the Ministry and the Spatial Data Board are working on high pressure in relation to handling the staff situation, and I would also like to say to Mrs Aaja Chemnitz Larsen that, to the extent that there should be competences in Greenland, we are, of course, also prepared to investigate it. This also applies to some of the other proposals that have been mentioned here today. There is a letter to the Greenland Committee that I do not know the content of, as I am not a member of the Greenland Committee, but if there are some options here, we are, of course, also prepared to look at it. It is also the case that new employees are recruited on an ongoing basis. The Spatial Data Board has so far employed four new employees for the production of Greenlandic charts, and a number of new posts have been opened for an early occupation. There is also a course in chart data for new employees with external assistance, including from some of the Spatial Data Authority's sister organisations, maritime partners and external consultants. There has been a course of educational assistance from the English sister organization. Training has been conducted with the assistance of the maritime partner, ICC Technologies, and there have also been a number of other initiatives to attract new employees, including from the UK, where there is a range of employees with these competencies. We'll look into it all. But it is also the case that there is a challenge associated with new employees, because you can't just start dealing with these charts overnight. This requires a great deal of specialist knowledge, and it requires a longer side-by-side training in order to handle that task. So all the possible steps are being taken to deal with the situation we are in. It doesn't change that we thought it was a real decision to move the Spatial Data Board to Aalborg. This is part of the relocation of approx. 4,000 state jobs with the aim of creating growth and development also in Denmark's outskirts. But it is clear that challenges have arisen here. There will certainly be other places. It is my job as Minister to address the challenge that is, as good as possible, and to fulfil the responsibilities that it is when you are a minister and in government, and to ensure that we do everything possible to deal with the situation that we are in as well as possible. I believe that we are doing this with the measures that I have just outlined. And if there are further proposals for action, we are also prepared to discuss them.
